Where the collected evidence is thin

11128 current archaeological, historical and interpretive records; 327 remain undated or contextual. Historical chronology review: 280 originally unplaced records reviewed; 64 source/period windows recovered and 5 disjoint windows separated. Historical totals are separate from current coverage. Natural-hazard catalogs are separate.

A zero is a collection gap, not evidence that no people lived there. Matrix values are distinct dated localities / regional or other context records. Expanded cells list local records, which may repeat phases at one locality. A local event does not necessarily show habitation. Undated records are reported separately. Broad date windows do not prove continuous habitation. Boxes and periods overlap deliberately; counts cannot be summed. Routes and records without point anchors are not allocated to a region.

Named U.S. state subregions are clipped to bundled 1934 state polygons when available, solely to identify modern geographic bins. No historical map boundaries are changed. The sparse ranking combines the dateline components of the Aleutians and of Fiji/Tonga/Samoa. Japan/Ryukyus falls outside the requested detailed ranking scope. The matrix retains the component rectangles for comparison.

Next-round priorities

Review linked regional/context records before ordering another literature search: some gaps need source or geographic validation rather than more papers. Ranked from current included evidence: sparse means zero, one or two distinct dated locality IDs. Regional/model context and undated findings cannot fill dated site gaps. Earliest peopling and habitability questions, including remote-island periods preceding supported settlement, require separate assessment and are not automatic collection-filling priorities. The screen is editorial, not evidence of human absence or a new first-arrival chronology.

Anangula Village — rows of houses above the water

Younger than the Anangula Core-and-Blade site

The plan evidence describes rows and approximate forms. Published descriptions leave spacing and contemporaneity unresolved. The map point refers to the island.

Anangula Village — rows of houses above the water
Chaluka — a stone-walled house and its work areas

Reported assays from material in the house: 4028 ± 100 to 3220 ± 52 radiocarbon BP

Six rock walls were identified at the site. Detailed observations refer to the excavated house. The associated samples leave the building’s calendar lifespan unresolved.

Chaluka — a stone-walled house and its work areas
Wanipigow Lake — early occupation beside forest waters

The provincial site designation estimates first evidence at approximately 6,000 years ago.

The early archaeological evidence is retained with its approximate age wording. The age is insufficient for a calendar onset or an occupation interval.

Wanipigow Lake — early occupation beside forest waters
Caniapiscau Lake — charcoal from an early pit

GaEl-3: 3485 ± 95 radiocarbon BP, on charcoal from a small pit feature.

The measurement establishes an early dated context in the basin. Its original units remain visible without a calendar placement.

Caniapiscau Lake — charcoal from an early pit
Mashteuiatsh — stone working and reused hearths

DdFb-18: reported 3475 ± 15 A.A. for a reused combustion feature and 725 ± 15 A.A. for a later combustion area.

The samples come from different features. The measurements indicate repeated work at the locality without filling the intervening centuries.

Mashteuiatsh — stone working and reused hearths
Whitefish Lake — cooking and return visits at Martin-Bird

The primary study reports Middle and Late Woodland components. Earlier charcoal age estimates run from the fifth to the eighteenth centuries CE, with significant cautions.

The broad set of dates from several features cannot establish a calendar span for the food residues or a continuous settlement.

Whitefish Lake — cooking and return visits at Martin-Bird
Caniapiscau — long dwellings with shared hearths

Recent Precontact period, about 1300–400 BP as reported for the region.

The structures show how camp space was organized. Their broad phase date leaves individual construction, use and rebuilding dates unresolved.

Caniapiscau — long dwellings with shared hearths
Waapushukamikw — the white hill’s toolstone quarries

Used for thousands of years according to the designation texts. Individual extraction dates are undetermined here.

The archaeological designations establish long use. The wording supplies neither a closed calendar window nor dates for separate quarry surfaces.

Waapushukamikw — the white hill’s toolstone quarries
Ashuanipi Lake — repeated camping at Ferguson Bay

Ferguson Bay 1 (FfDn-01) has evidence at least as old as 1640 ± 50 radiocarbon BP (Beta-226315). A later precontact hearth is reported at about 1000 radiocarbon BP.

The earlier measurement is an age constraint. The later feature is a separate episode of use. The original radiocarbon dates remain in their reported units.

Ashuanipi Lake — repeated camping at Ferguson Bay
Miskweyaabiziibee — camps and quartz along the Bloodvein

Archaeological components from precontact through the fur-trade period. Quarry use was not individually dated.

Many sites were recorded at different points on the river. The observations cannot be placed in one continuous occupation window.

Miskweyaabiziibee — camps and quartz along the Bloodvein
Sipiwesk Lake — how potters found clay

Middle and Late Woodland pottery, as assigned in the study. The consulted abstract does not give site-specific calendar endpoints.

The materials identify choices about where clay was obtained. No individual residence interval is established.

Sipiwesk Lake — how potters found clay
Metepenagiag — Augustine Mound ceremonial and exchange evidence

Community account: cemetery dates back to before 600 BCE; official heritage account: about 2,500 years ago

No timeline year assigned: the community source gives an open-ended date before 600 BCE, while Parks Canada gives a rounded age of about 2,500 years. Neither is silently transformed into an exact construction year.

Metepenagiag — Augustine Mound ceremonial and exchange evidence
Later northern gene flow and back-migration — unresolved timing

After 11.5 ka; no closed interval provided

A one-sided or relative temporal constraint is meaningful but does not establish a closed event interval. Preserve the constraint prominently; show separate regional context rather than fabricate the missing edge.

Later northern gene flow and back-migration — unresolved timing
